The bombolone is the filled doughnut of Tuscany, now loved all over Italy. It is a close relative of the Krapfen from Austria and South Tyrol, which spread through northern Italy; in Tuscany the same idea took its own name. The word comes from bomba, for its round, puffed-up shape.

### How it is made

Traditionally, a soft enriched yeast dough is left to rise, cut into rounds and fried until golden. While still warm, the bomboloni are rolled in sugar and then filled through the side, the classic filling being crema pasticcera, Italian pastry cream. Packaged versions bring the same idea to the shelf, ready to open whenever the craving hits.

### Curiosities

- On Italian beaches in summer, vendors walk along the sand calling out bomboloni to sunbathers.
- Unlike a ring doughnut, a bombolone has no hole: the filling needs somewhere to go.
- Cream is the Tuscan classic, but chocolate and jam versions are just as common in Italian bars.

### How to enjoy it

Pair it with a cappuccino for a proper Italian breakfast, or with an espresso in the afternoon. A few seconds in a warm oven make it softer and more fragrant. Eat it by hand, with a napkin close by.7b:T508,### Waar het vandaan komt

Traditioneel laat je een zacht, verrijkt gistdeeg rijzen, steek je er rondjes uit en bak je die goudbruin. The artichoke is one of the great vegetables of Italian cooking. Rome has its carciofi alla romana and alla giudia, while Sardinia, Sicily, Puglia and Tuscany each have their own varieties and dishes. In spring, markets across the country are piled high with them. An artichoke cream brings that flavour to the table all year round.

### How it is made
Vegetable creams of this kind are usually made by cooking artichoke hearts until tender and blending them into a smooth, spreadable paste, which is then sealed in jars. The tough outer leaves come off first, since only the tender inner part is soft enough to become a cream.

### Curiosities
- The artichoke is actually the flower bud of a thistle; left on the plant, it opens into a violet flower.
- Artichokes contain cynarin, a compound that can make whatever you taste next, even water, seem slightly sweet.
- Italy is one of the largest artichoke producers in the world.

### How to enjoy it
Spread it on crostini or warm bread, add a spoonful to a panino with cured meats or cheese, or loosen it with a little pasta cooking water for a quick, creamy pasta sauce.84:T498,### Waar het vandaan komt

- Italië is een van de grootste artisjokproducenten ter wereld.

Il carciofo è uno dei grandi ortaggi della cucina italiana. Roma ha i suoi carciofi alla romana e alla giudia, mentre Sardegna, Sicilia, Puglia e Toscana hanno ciascuna le proprie varietà e ricette. In primavera i mercati di tutto il Paese ne sono pieni. Una crema di carciofi porta quel sapore in tavola tutto l'anno.

### Come si fa
Le creme di questo tipo si preparano di solito cuocendo i cuori di carciofo finché sono teneri e frullandoli in una crema liscia e spalmabile, poi chiusa in vasetto. Prima si tolgono le foglie esterne più dure, perché solo la parte interna è abbastanza tenera per diventare crema.

### Curiosità
- Il carciofo è in realtà il bocciolo di un cardo: se resta sulla pianta, si apre in un fiore viola.
- Contiene cinarina, una sostanza che fa sembrare leggermente dolce ciò che si assaggia subito dopo, perfino l'acqua.
- L'Italia è tra i maggiori produttori di carciofi al mondo.

### Come gustarlo

The Sorrento peninsula, on the southern side of the Gulf of Naples, is famous for its citrus: terraced groves that run down towards the sea. Citrus has shaped both the landscape and the kitchen of the area for generations, from limoncello to candied peel and marmalades, and mandarins are part of that story.

### How it is made
Citrus marmalade is made by cooking the fruit, usually with part of its peel, with sugar until it sets into a glossy preserve, which is then sealed in jars. The peel brings a pleasant bitterness and fragrance that balance the sweetness of the fruit.

### Curiosities
- Under EU rules, the word marmellata is reserved for citrus preserves, so a mandarin preserve is rightly called marmellata.
- On the peninsula, citrus groves have traditionally been sheltered by wooden frames and straw mats, called pagliarelle, against wind and cold.
- Mandarins are small, sweet and aromatic, with a peel especially rich in fragrant oils.

### How to enjoy it

Vatolla is a small hilltop village in the Cilento, the green, rugged stretch of Campania south of Salerno. The Cilento is known for a traditional way of eating based on vegetables, legumes, olive oil and bread, the kind of cooking later studied as the Mediterranean diet. Onions are part of that everyday kitchen, and this cream carries the name of the village.

### How it is made
Onion creams of this kind are made by cooking onions slowly until they soften and turn sweet, then blending them into a smooth, spreadable paste that is sealed in jars. Slow cooking is what takes away the sharpness of raw onion and brings out its natural sugars.

### Curiosities
- The philosopher Giambattista Vico spent several years in Vatolla as a young tutor, living in the village castle.
- The American scientist Ancel Keys, who made the idea of the Mediterranean diet famous, lived for many years in the Cilento.
- Onions have been grown around the Mediterranean since antiquity.

Nduja is a soft, spreadable salume from Calabria, the toe of the Italian boot. It is most closely associated with the area around Spilinga, in the province of Vibo Valentia, and grew out of the peasant habit of letting nothing of the pig go to waste. Its fiery red colour comes from Calabrian chilli pepper, a signature of the region's cooking.

### How it is made
Traditionally, pork is minced very finely and worked with a generous amount of ground chilli and salt. The mixture is stuffed into a natural casing, lightly smoked and left to cure. The result stays soft, almost creamy, instead of drying out into a salami you can slice. In a jar, it is ready to spoon out and use.

### Curiosities
- The name is widely thought to come from the French andouille, a sausage that reached southern Italy centuries ago.
- Nduja softens at room temperature and melts in a warm pan, which is why cooks use it as a seasoning as much as a spread.

### How to enjoy it

Sun-drying tomatoes is an old southern Italian way of keeping the summer harvest. In Puglia, Calabria, Sicily and Campania, tomatoes were split open, salted and laid out in the late summer sun, then stored in oil for the winter. A cream of sun-dried tomatoes turns that intense, concentrated flavour into something you can spread.

### How it is made
For the dried tomatoes, ripe fruit is halved, lightly salted and dried until most of the water has gone, which concentrates sugar, acidity and savoury flavour. For a cream, the dried tomatoes are then blended into a smooth paste and sealed in jars.

### Curiosities
- It takes a large quantity of fresh tomatoes to make a small amount of dried ones, since most of the fruit is water.
- Drying brings out a deep, savoury taste that fresh tomatoes do not have.
- Sun-dried tomatoes were a practical pantry food long before they became a fashionable ingredient abroad.

### How to enjoy it

Fig trees grow all around the Mediterranean, and in southern Italy they are part of the landscape: in Calabria, Puglia, Sicily and the Cilento, late summer brings more ripe figs than anyone can eat fresh. Turning them into jam, or drying them in the sun, has long been the way to keep them for the colder months.

### How it is made
A fig confettura is made by cooking the fruit with sugar until it thickens into a soft, spoonable preserve, which is then sealed in jars. Figs are naturally rich in sugar, and their tiny seeds stay in the jam and give it a gentle crunch.

- The fig is one of the oldest cultivated fruit trees, grown around the Mediterranean since antiquity.
- What we eat as a fig is, botanically, a hollow cluster of tiny flowers turned inward.

Caffè d'orzo, a drink made from roasted barley, has been part of Italian kitchens for generations. In the countryside barley was cheap and grown locally, so roasting it in a pan over the fire gave families a dark, warm drink when real coffee was too expensive. It became especially common in the years around the two world wars, when coffee was scarce, and it never left: many Italian bars still offer orzo next to espresso.

### How it is made
Barley grains are roasted until they turn deep brown and smell of toast and caramel, then ground. For an instant version like this one, the roasted barley is brewed and the resulting liquid is dried into a soluble powder, so it dissolves straight into hot water or milk without a filter or a machine.

### Curiosities
- At an Italian bar you can order “orzo in tazza grande” or “orzo in tazza piccola”, a large or a small cup, just as you would a coffee.
- Many bars make orzo with the espresso machine, using special capsules or pods.
- Orzo with hot milk is a classic Italian breakfast, often with a biscuit to dip in.

### How to enjoy it
Stir a spoonful or two into a cup of hot water or hot milk, adjust the strength to your taste and add sugar or honey if you like. It is lovely in the afternoon or the evening, with biscuits or a slice of cake.96:T547,### Waar het vandaan komt

Gerstekorrels worden geroosterd tot ze diepbruin zijn en naar toast en karamel ruiken, en daarna gemalen. Aubergines preserved in oil belong to the home kitchens of southern Italy, from Campania and Calabria to Puglia and Sicily. At the end of summer, when aubergines are plentiful and cheap, many families still spend a day preparing jars to keep for the rest of the year.

### How it is made
Traditionally the aubergines are often peeled and cut into thin strips, then salted and pressed to draw out their bitter water. They are cooked briefly in water and vinegar, squeezed well and layered in jars with garlic, chilli and oregano or mint, then covered with oil. The vinegar and the oil give them their soft texture and bright, tangy taste.

### Curiosities
- Cutting the aubergine into strips, "a filetti", is the most common style in the south.
- Pressing is a key step: the drier the aubergine goes into the jar, the better it keeps its texture.
- In many southern families the jarring of aubergines is a shared late-summer ritual, alongside tomato sauce.

### How to enjoy it
They are a classic in a panino, with cured meats or a soft cheese, and on an antipasto board. Try them on bruschetta, next to grilled meat or mixed into a pasta salad.99:T4ed,### Waar het vandaan komt
Aubergines op olie horen bij de thuiskeukens van Zuid-Italië, van Campanië en Calabrië tot Apulië en Sicilië. Aan het eind van de zomer, als er volop goedkope aubergines zijn, trekken veel families nog altijd een dag uit om potten te maken voor de rest van het jaar.

### Hoe het gemaakt wordt
Traditioneel worden de aubergines vaak geschild en in dunne reepjes gesneden, gezouten en aangedrukt zodat het bittere vocht eruit gaat. Daarna worden ze kort gekookt in water met azijn, goed uitgeknepen en laag voor laag in potten gelegd met knoflook, chilipeper en oregano of munt, en afgedekt met olie. Azijn en olie geven ze hun zachte structuur en frisse, pittige smaak.

### Weetjes
- De aubergine in reepjes snijden, "a filetti", is in het zuiden de meest gebruikelijke manier.
- Het aandrukken is een belangrijke stap: hoe droger de aubergine de pot in gaat, hoe beter ze haar structuur houdt.
- In veel Zuid-Italiaanse families is het inmaken van aubergines een gezamenlijk ritueel aan het eind van de zomer, net als tomatensaus.

Le melanzane sott'olio appartengono alle cucine di casa del Sud Italia, dalla Campania e dalla Calabria fino alla Puglia e alla Sicilia. A fine estate, quando le melanzane sono abbondanti e costano poco, molte famiglie dedicano ancora una giornata a preparare i vasetti per il resto dell'anno.

### Come si fa

Italy is one of the world's largest artichoke growers, with fields in Sicily, Puglia, Sardinia, Campania and Lazio. Artichokes are a winter and spring vegetable, and preserving them in oil is the traditional way to keep them on the table all year, above all as part of an antipasto.

### How it is made
Traditionally the artichokes are trimmed down to the tender heart, cooked briefly in water and vinegar and drained. For the grilled version they are then cooked on a grill or over embers, which gives them their smoky, lightly charred edges, before being packed into jars and covered with oil, often with herbs or garlic.

### Curiosities
- Catherine de' Medici is said to have loved artichokes so much that she helped make them fashionable at the French court.
- In Rome, carciofi alla romana and carciofi alla giudia are among the best-known dishes of the city.
- Only the inner, tender part of the artichoke is kept for preserving: much of the outside is trimmed away.

### How to enjoy it

Chilli peppers reached Europe from the Americas in the sixteenth century and found a second home in southern Italy. In Calabria and Basilicata above all, peperoncino became part of everyday cooking, and preserving it in oil is one of the simplest ways to keep its heat on the table all year.

### How it is made
Traditionally the chillies are washed, dried, cut into rings or pieces and left with salt for a while to lose some of their water. They are then pressed into jars and covered with oil, sometimes with a little garlic or herbs, depending on the family recipe. Over time the oil takes on the colour and warmth of the chilli.

### Curiosities
- The town of Diamante, on the Calabrian coast, hosts a well-known festival dedicated entirely to peperoncino.
- The oil in the jar is a condiment in its own right: a few drops are enough to give heat to a dish.
- In southern kitchens a jar of chilli in oil often sits next to the salt and olive oil, ready for every meal.

### How to enjoy it

Chilipepers kwamen in de zestiende eeuw vanuit Amerika naar Europa en vonden in Zuid-Italië een tweede thuis. ### Hoe het gemaakt wordt

Daarna gaan ze stevig aangedrukt in potten en worden ze met olie afgedekt, soms met wat knoflook of kruiden, afhankelijk van het familierecept. Colatura di alici is an amber anchovy sauce from the Amalfi Coast, most closely linked to the fishing village of Cetara. It is often described as a descendant of garum, the fish sauce the ancient Romans used as a seasoning, and it has become one of the best-known flavours of that coast.

### How it is made
Traditionally, anchovies caught in spring are cleaned and layered with sea salt in wooden barrels, then pressed under a weight. Over the following months they release a liquid, which at the end of the maturing is let through the fish and collected: "colatura" literally means "what drips through". The result is a clear, concentrated liquid, intense and savoury.

### Curiosities
- On the Amalfi Coast, spaghetti with colatura is a traditional dish for Christmas Eve.
- The small wooden barrels used for the salting are traditionally called terzigni.

### How to enjoy it
Use it as a seasoning, not as a sauce: a few drops are enough. The classic is spaghetti tossed with olive oil, garlic, parsley and a spoonful of colatura, with little or no salt in the pasta water, because the colatura brings its own. It also lifts boiled potatoes, steamed vegetables, escarole and raw or grilled fish.a2:T512,### Waar het vandaan komt
Colatura di alici is een amberkleurige ansjovissaus van de Amalfikust, vooral verbonden met het vissersdorp Cetara. Ze wordt vaak gezien als een afstammeling van garum, de vissaus waarmee de oude Romeinen hun eten op smaak brachten, en is een van de bekendste smaken van die kust geworden.

### Hoe het gemaakt wordt
Traditioneel worden ansjovissen die in het voorjaar gevangen zijn schoongemaakt en laag voor laag met zeezout in houten vaatjes gelegd, met een gewicht erop. In de maanden daarna geven ze vocht af, dat aan het eind van de rijping door de vis heen sijpelt en wordt opgevangen: "colatura" betekent letterlijk "wat erdoorheen druppelt". Het resultaat is een heldere, geconcentreerde vloeistof, intens en hartig.

### Weetjes
- Aan de Amalfikust is spaghetti met colatura een traditioneel gerecht op kerstavond.
- De kleine houten vaatjes waarin de ansjovis wordt gezouten heten traditioneel terzigni.

Friarielli is the Neapolitan name for the tender shoots of a leafy green of the turnip family, known elsewhere in Italy as cime di rapa or broccoli di rapa. They are one of the signature vegetables of Naples and Campania, loved for a pleasant bitterness that sets them apart from other greens.

### How it is made
In Naples, friarielli are traditionally cleaned by hand, keeping only the tender leaves, stems and flower buds, then cooked in a pan with olive oil, garlic and chilli until soft. This way of cooking them is known as "in padella". Preserved in oil, they are ready to use whenever you want them, without the cleaning and cooking.

### Curiosities
- Salsiccia e friarielli, sausage with friarielli, is one of the best-known pairings in Neapolitan cooking: in a pan, in a panino or on pizza.
- The name is often said to come from the Neapolitan word for frying, a reference to how they are cooked.
- In Naples the name friarielli is used for these greens, while elsewhere in Italy the same word can mean small frying peppers.

### How to enjoy it

Drying tomatoes is an old habit of southern Italy, from Puglia and Calabria to Sicily and Campania, where the late-summer sun is strong and reliable. It was a way to keep the summer harvest for the months when fresh tomatoes were gone, and dried tomatoes in oil are still a fixture of the southern pantry.

### How it is made
Traditionally, ripe tomatoes are cut in half, salted and laid out to dry in the sun for several days, until they lose most of their water. The dried tomatoes are then often rinsed or briefly blanched in water and vinegar, dried again and packed into jars with oil and herbs such as oregano, sometimes with garlic or chilli.

### Curiosities
- It takes many kilos of fresh tomatoes to make a single kilo of dried ones, which is why their flavour is so concentrated.
- Traditionally the halves were spread on wooden trays or mats in the open air and brought in at night.

### How to enjoy it
Chop them into pasta, salads and focaccia, lay them in a panino with mozzarella or cured meat, or serve them with bread as part of an antipasto. The oil in the jar is good for dressing too, from a salad to a plate of pasta.a8:T4c5,### Waar het vandaan komt

Artisjokken zijn winter- en voorjaarsgroente, en ze op olie inmaken is de traditionele manier om ze het hele jaar op tafel te hebben, vooral als onderdeel van een antipasto.

### Hoe het gemaakt wordt
Traditioneel worden de artisjokken schoongemaakt tot op het zachte hart, kort gekookt in water met azijn en uitgelekt. Voor de gegrilde versie gaan ze daarna op de grill of boven gloeiende kolen, wat ze hun rokerige, licht geblakerde randjes geeft. Daarna gaan ze in potten, afgedekt met olie, vaak met kruiden of knoflook.

### Weetjes
- Catharina de' Medici zou zo dol op artisjokken zijn geweest dat ze hielp ze aan het Franse hof in de mode te brengen.
- In Rome horen carciofi alla romana en carciofi alla giudia bij de bekendste gerechten van de stad.
- Alleen het binnenste, zachte deel van de artisjok wordt ingemaakt: een groot deel van de buitenkant wordt weggesneden.

### Zo geniet je ervan
Serveer ze zo uit de pot als antipasto met vleeswaren en kaas, snijd ze in een panino of een salade, of schep ze op het laatste moment door de pasta. Een beetje van hun olie is lekker om het gerecht mee af te maken.af:T4cb,### Da dove viene
L'Italia è tra i maggiori produttori di carciofi al mondo, con coltivazioni in Sicilia, Puglia, Sardegna, Campania e Lazio. Il carciofo è un ortaggio d'inverno e di primavera, e conservarlo sott'olio è il modo tradizionale per averlo in tavola tutto l'anno, soprattutto nell'antipasto.

### Come si fa
Tradizionalmente i carciofi vengono puliti fino al cuore tenero, sbollentati in acqua e aceto e scolati. Per la versione grigliata vengono poi cotti sulla griglia o sulla brace, che dà loro i bordi affumicati e leggermente abbrustoliti, prima di essere sistemati nei vasetti e coperti d'olio, spesso con erbe o aglio.

### Curiosità
- Si racconta che Caterina de' Medici amasse così tanto i carciofi da contribuire a renderli di moda alla corte di Francia.
- A Roma i carciofi alla romana e i carciofi alla giudia sono tra i piatti più noti della città.
- Per la conserva si tiene solo la parte interna e tenera del carciofo: gran parte dell'esterno viene eliminata.

### Come gustarlo
Servili direttamente dal vasetto come antipasto con salumi e formaggi, tagliali in un panino o in un'insalata, oppure uniscili alla pasta all'ultimo momento. Un po' del loro olio è perfetto per condire il piatto.b0:T4a6,### Where it comes from
Olive ammaccate, also called olive schiacciate, are crushed olives, a home preserve found across southern Italy, from Cilento in Campania to Calabria and Puglia. They are traditionally made in autumn, when the green olives are picked, and many families still prepare their own jars every year.

### How it is made
Traditionally each olive is cracked with a stone or a wooden mallet, leaving the stone inside. The olives are then soaked in water, changed every day for a period, to remove their natural bitterness. Once ready, they are drained, salted and dressed with ingredients such as garlic, chilli, wild fennel or oregano, and covered with oil.

### Curiosities
- Cracking the olive lets the water reach the flesh, so the bitterness goes much faster than with whole olives.
- "Ammaccate" means bruised or dented, a direct description of how they look.
- Every family and village tends to have its own mix of herbs and spices for dressing them.

### How to enjoy it
Serve them with an aperitivo, with bread, cheese and cured meats, or add them to salads, a tomato sauce or a dish of baked fish. They are also good chopped over a simple pasta with oil and garlic.b1:T52f,### Waar het vandaan komt
Olive ammaccate, ook olive schiacciate genoemd, zijn gekneusde olijven: een huisgemaakte conserve die je overal in Zuid-Italië vindt, van Cilento in Campanië tot Calabrië en Apulië. Traditioneel worden ze in de herfst gemaakt, als de groene olijven worden geplukt, en veel families maken nog elk jaar hun eigen potten.

### Hoe het gemaakt wordt
Traditioneel wordt elke olijf met een steen of een houten hamer opengebroken, met de pit er nog in. Daarna weken de olijven een tijd in water dat elke dag wordt ververst, om hun natuurlijke bitterheid te verliezen. Als ze klaar zijn, worden ze uitgelekt, gezouten en op smaak gebracht met bijvoorbeeld knoflook, chilipeper, wilde venkel of oregano, en afgedekt met olie.

### Weetjes
- Door de olijf open te breken bereikt het water het vruchtvlees, zodat de bitterheid veel sneller verdwijnt dan bij hele olijven.
- "Ammaccate" betekent gekneusd of gedeukt, een letterlijke beschrijving van hoe ze eruitzien.
- Elke familie en elk dorp heeft meestal een eigen mengsel van kruiden en specerijen om ze op smaak te brengen.

Le olive ammaccate, chiamate anche olive schiacciate, sono una conserva di casa diffusa in tutto il Sud Italia, dal Cilento in Campania fino alla Calabria e alla Puglia. Tradizionalmente si preparano in autunno, quando si raccolgono le olive verdi, e molte famiglie fanno ancora i propri vasetti ogni anno.

- Ogni famiglia e ogni paese ha di solito il proprio mix di erbe e spezie per condirle.

Anchovies, alici in Italian, are among the most important fish of the Mediterranean, and Italy has a long tradition of preserving them, from the Amalfi Coast and Sicily to Liguria. Salted anchovies and anchovy fillets in oil are staples of the Italian pantry, used as much for seasoning as for eating on their own.

### How it is made
Traditionally, fresh anchovies are cleaned and layered with salt, then left to mature for several months. The maturing is what gives them their firm texture and deep, savoury taste. Afterwards they are rinsed, filleted and covered with oil; in this jar, with extra virgin olive oil.

### Curiosities
- In Piedmont, far from the sea, anchovies are the base of bagna cauda, a warm dip of garlic, anchovies and oil served with vegetables.
- Warmed gently in oil, anchovies melt completely, which is why many Italian sauces use them as a hidden seasoning.
- Anchovies were for centuries one of the cheapest ways to bring the taste of the sea to inland kitchens.

### How to enjoy it
Lay a fillet on bread and butter or on bruschetta, add them to pizza, puttanesca sauce or a green salad, or melt a couple in oil with garlic as the base for pasta with broccoli or toasted breadcrumbs. The oil from the jar can go into the pan as well.b4:T567,### Waar het vandaan komt

Alici sotto sale e filetti di alici sott'olio sono una presenza fissa nella dispensa italiana, usati tanto per insaporire quanto da gustare così.

### Come si fa

Vatolla is a small hilltop village in Cilento, the southern part of Campania, within the municipality of Perdifumo. Its name is attached to a local onion grown in the area and used in Cilento home cooking. Cilento itself is a land of olive groves, vegetable gardens and a pantry built on preserves, from olives to vegetables in oil.

### How it is made
Onions in oil follow the same old logic as other southern vegetable preserves. Traditionally they are peeled and sliced, cooked briefly in water and vinegar, drained and dried well, then packed into jars and covered with oil, often with herbs. The vinegar keeps their flavour bright and the oil keeps them tender.

### Curiosities
- The philosopher Giambattista Vico spent several years in Vatolla as a young man, working as a tutor for a local family.
- Cilento is also where the American scientist Ancel Keys studied what became known as the Mediterranean diet.

### How to enjoy it
Serve them with cheese and cured meats on an antipasto board, tuck them into a panino, put them next to grilled meat or fish, or chop them over a salad or a plate of beans. A little of their oil is good on bread too.b7:T4e1,### Waar het vandaan komt

Zucchine alla scapece is a classic of Naples and Campania, eaten as an antipasto or side dish, especially in summer. "Scapece" is one of several Mediterranean names for food dressed and preserved with vinegar, and it is usually linked to the Spanish escabeche.

### How it is made
In the classic home recipe, zucchini are sliced into rounds and fried in oil until golden, then layered with garlic, fresh mint and a splash of vinegar and left to rest for a few hours. Vinegar and mint are what make a dish "alla scapece". A jar brings this Neapolitan style to the table ready to serve, without any frying.

### Curiosities
- The dish is traditionally considered better the next day, once the flavours have had time to settle in.
- The same method is used for other vegetables and for small fish, which are also served "a scapece".
- In Naples it is a typical dish to prepare ahead for a Sunday lunch or a day at the beach.

### How to enjoy it
Serve at room temperature as an antipasto, next to grilled meat or fish, with a fresh cheese such as mozzarella, or in a panino with bread that can soak up the dressing.ba:T4b4,### Waar het vandaan komt
Zucchine alla scapece is een klassieker uit Napels en Campanië, gegeten als antipasto of bijgerecht, vooral in de zomer. "Scapece" is een van de Mediterrane namen voor eten dat met azijn wordt aangemaakt en bewaard, en wordt meestal in verband gebracht met het Spaanse escabeche.

### Hoe het gemaakt wordt
In het klassieke thuisrecept wordt courgette in plakjes gesneden en in olie goudbruin gebakken, daarna laag voor laag gelegd met knoflook, verse munt en een scheutje azijn en een paar uur weggezet. Azijn en munt maken een gerecht "alla scapece". Een pot brengt deze Napolitaanse stijl kant-en-klaar op tafel, zonder te bakken.

### Weetjes
- Traditioneel vindt men het gerecht de volgende dag lekkerder, als de smaken de tijd hebben gehad om in te trekken.
- Dezelfde methode wordt gebruikt voor andere groenten en voor kleine visjes, die ook "a scapece" worden geserveerd.
- In Napels is het een typisch gerecht om vooruit te maken voor de zondagse lunch of een dag aan het strand.

Le zucchine alla scapece sono un classico di Napoli e della Campania, servite come antipasto o contorno, soprattutto d'estate. "Scapece" è uno dei nomi mediterranei per i cibi conditi e conservati con l'aceto, e di solito viene collegato allo spagnolo escabeche.

### Come si fa
Nella ricetta di casa le zucchine si tagliano a rondelle e si friggono nell'olio fino a dorarle, poi si sistemano a strati con aglio, menta fresca e un filo d'aceto e si lasciano riposare qualche ora. Pesto alla genovese comes from Liguria and its capital, Genoa, on the Italian Riviera. It is built around basil, which grows especially well in the mild Ligurian climate, and it has become one of the best-known Italian sauces in the world.

### How it is made
The classic recipe pounds fresh basil leaves in a marble mortar with a wooden pestle, together with garlic, pine nuts, coarse salt, grated aged cheese and extra virgin olive oil. The name comes from pestare, "to pound". Ready-made pesto follows the same idea, blending everything into a smooth green sauce, and each producer has its own recipe.

### Curiosities
- Genoa hosts a world championship of pesto made in the mortar.
- Genovese basil has small, tender, very fragrant leaves and is protected by its own designation of origin.
- Pesto is never cooked: heat dulls the colour and the fresh aroma of the basil.

### How to enjoy it
Toss it with trofie or trenette, traditionally with boiled potatoes and green beans cooked in the same pot as the pasta. Loosen it with a spoonful of pasta water and add it off the heat. It is also good on bruschetta, in minestrone or in a mozzarella sandwich.bd:T4ec,### Waar het vandaan komt
Pesto alla genovese komt uit Ligurië en de hoofdstad Genua, aan de Italiaanse Rivièra. Het draait om basilicum, dat in het milde Ligurische klimaat bijzonder goed groeit, en het is uitgegroeid tot een van de bekendste Italiaanse sauzen ter wereld.

### Hoe het gemaakt wordt
Voor het klassieke recept worden verse basilicumblaadjes in een marmeren vijzel met een houten stamper fijngestampt, samen met knoflook, pijnboompitten, grof zout, geraspte belegen kaas en extra vierge olijfolie. De naam komt van pestare, "stampen". Kant-en-klare pesto volgt hetzelfde idee en mengt alles tot een gladde groene saus; elke producent heeft zijn eigen recept.

### Weetjes
- Genua organiseert een wereldkampioenschap pesto uit de vijzel.
- Genovese basilicum heeft kleine, zachte, heel geurige blaadjes en heeft een eigen beschermde oorsprongsbenaming.
- Pesto wordt nooit gekookt: hitte maakt de kleur doffer en haalt het frisse aroma van de basilicum weg.

### Zo geniet je ervan
Schep het door trofie of trenette, traditioneel met aardappel en sperziebonen die in dezelfde pan als de pasta zijn gekookt. Maak het los met een lepel kookwater en voeg het van het vuur toe. Ook lekker op bruschetta, in minestrone of op een broodje mozzarella.be:T4bb,### Da dove viene
Il pesto alla genovese viene dalla Liguria e dal suo capoluogo, Genova, sulla Riviera. Nasce intorno al basilico, che nel clima mite ligure cresce particolarmente bene, ed è diventato uno dei sughi italiani più conosciuti al mondo.

### Come si fa
La ricetta classica prevede di pestare le foglie di basilico fresco in un mortaio di marmo con un pestello di legno, insieme ad aglio, pinoli, sale grosso, formaggio stagionato grattugiato e olio extravergine d'oliva. Il nome viene proprio da pestare. Il pesto pronto segue la stessa idea, frullando tutto in una salsa verde e liscia, e ogni produttore ha la sua ricetta.

### Curiosità
- Genova ospita un campionato mondiale di pesto al mortaio.
- Il basilico genovese ha foglie piccole, tenere e molto profumate, ed è tutelato da una propria denominazione di origine.
- Il pesto non si cuoce mai: il calore spegne il colore e il profumo fresco del basilico.

Fresella, also called frisella, is a ring of bread from the south of Italy, especially Campania and Puglia. It was the bread of farmers, fishermen and travellers: because it is baked twice until completely dry, it could be kept for weeks and softened whenever it was needed. Summer lunches in the south still revolve around it.

### How it is made
A bread dough is shaped into rings and baked. The rings are then cut in half horizontally and baked again, slowly, until they are dry and hard right through. This second baking is what gives fresella its long life and its rough, porous cut side, which drinks up water, oil and tomato juice. “Grossa” simply means the large size.

### Curiosities
- A common story says fishermen softened their freselle by dipping them in sea water, which seasoned them at the same time.
- The hole in the middle was practical: freselle could be threaded on a string and hung up.
- In Naples the topped fresella is the base of “caponata napoletana”, nothing like the Sicilian aubergine caponata.

### How to enjoy it
Dip it quickly in cold water, just long enough to soften the surface, and let it drain. Top it with chopped ripe tomatoes, salt, oregano and plenty of olive oil, and add basil, tuna or mozzarella if you like. It is a lunch in itself.c3:T570,### Waar het vandaan komt
Fresella, ook frisella genoemd, is een ring van brood uit Zuid-Italië, vooral uit Campanië en Apulië. Het was het brood van boeren, vissers en reizigers: omdat het twee keer wordt gebakken tot het helemaal droog is, kon het wekenlang bewaard worden en weer zacht gemaakt wanneer dat nodig was. Zomerse lunches in het zuiden draaien er nog altijd om.

### Hoe het wordt gemaakt
Brooddeeg wordt tot ringen gevormd en gebakken. Daarna worden de ringen horizontaal doorgesneden en nog een keer langzaam gebakken, tot ze door en door droog en hard zijn. Die tweede bakronde geeft de fresella haar lange houdbaarheid en haar ruwe, poreuze snijkant, die water, olie en tomatensap opzuigt. “Grossa” betekent simpelweg het grote formaat.

### Weetjes
- Volgens een bekend verhaal weekten vissers hun freselle in zeewater, waardoor ze meteen op smaak waren.
- Het gat in het midden was handig: je kon freselle aan een touw rijgen en ophangen.
- In Napels is de belegde fresella de basis van de “caponata napoletana”, iets heel anders dan de Siciliaanse caponata met aubergine.

### Zo geniet je ervan
Haal hem kort door koud water, net lang genoeg om de bovenkant zacht te maken, en laat hem uitlekken. Beleg met gehakte rijpe tomaten, zout, oregano en flink wat olijfolie, en voeg naar wens basilicum, tonijn of mozzarella toe. Zo heb je meteen een complete lunch.c4:T563,### Da dove viene
La fresella, o frisella, è un anello di pane del Sud Italia, soprattutto di Campania e Puglia. Era il pane di contadini, pescatori e viaggiatori: cotta due volte fino a seccarsi del tutto, si conservava per settimane e si ammorbidiva quando serviva. Ancora oggi è al centro dei pranzi estivi del Sud.

### Come si fa
L'impasto di pane viene modellato ad anello e cotto. Poi gli anelli si tagliano a metà in orizzontale e tornano in forno, a lungo e a bassa temperatura, finché sono secchi e duri fino al cuore. È questa seconda cottura a dare alla fresella la sua lunga conservazione e la superficie ruvida e porosa del taglio, che assorbe acqua, olio e succo di pomodoro. “Grossa” indica semplicemente il formato grande.

### Curiosità
- Si racconta che i pescatori bagnassero le freselle nell'acqua di mare, che le condiva allo stesso tempo.
- Il buco al centro era pratico: le freselle si infilavano in uno spago e si appendevano.
- A Napoli la fresella condita è la base della “caponata napoletana”, tutt'altra cosa rispetto alla caponata siciliana di melanzane.

### Come gustarlo
Passala velocemente in acqua fredda, giusto il tempo di ammorbidire la superficie, e lasciala sgocciolare. Condiscila con pomodori maturi a pezzetti, sale, origano e abbondante olio d'oliva, e aggiungi a piacere basilico, tonno o mozzarella. È già un pranzo.c5:T524,### Where it comes from
Taralli are the ring-shaped savoury biscuits of southern Italy, and Naples has its own famous version. The story usually told is that eighteenth-century bakers, rather than waste the scraps of bread dough, worked them with pepper and fat, twisted them into rings and baked them. Street sellers, the tarallari, carried them in baskets through the city and along the seafront, keeping them warm under a cloth.

### How it is made
The classic Neapolitan tarallo, known as “sugna e pepe”, is made from a peppery dough rolled into strands, twisted together and closed into a ring, often with almonds pressed into the surface. The rings are baked slowly until they turn golden and crumbly. Recipes vary from one maker to another, so the pack is the place to check what goes into these.

### Curiosities
- In Italian, “finire a tarallucci e vino”, to end with taralli and wine, means settling an argument over a friendly drink.
- A walk along the Naples seafront was traditionally rounded off with a warm tarallo and a cold beer.
- Taralli were made to keep: their dry, crisp texture lets them last for weeks in a tin.

### How to enjoy it
Serve them with an aperitivo, a glass of wine or a cold beer, next to olives and cured meats. Crumble one over a salad or dip it in a little olive oil.c6:T582,### Waar het vandaan komt
Taralli zijn de ringvormige hartige koekjes van Zuid-Italië, en Napels heeft een eigen, beroemde versie. Het verhaal gaat dat bakkers in de achttiende eeuw de restjes brooddeeg niet wilden weggooien: ze werkten ze door met peper en vet, draaiden er ringen van en bakten ze. Straatverkopers, de tarallari, droegen ze in manden door de stad en langs de boulevard, warm gehouden onder een doek.

### Hoe het wordt gemaakt
De klassieke Napolitaanse tarallo, bekend als “sugna e pepe”, wordt gemaakt van een peperig deeg dat in strengen wordt gerold, in elkaar gedraaid en tot een ring gesloten, vaak met amandelen in het oppervlak gedrukt. De ringen worden langzaam gebakken tot ze goudbruin en bros zijn. Elke maker heeft zijn eigen recept, dus wat er in deze taralli zit, lees je op de verpakking.

### Weetjes
- “Finire a tarallucci e vino”, eindigen met taralli en wijn, betekent in het Italiaans een ruzie bijleggen onder het genot van een drankje.
- Een wandeling langs de boulevard van Napels werd traditioneel afgesloten met een warme tarallo en een koud biertje.
- Taralli zijn gemaakt om te bewaren: door hun droge, knapperige structuur blijven ze in een blik wekenlang goed.

### Zo geniet je ervan
Zet ze op tafel bij het aperitief, een glas wijn of een koud biertje, naast olijven en vleeswaren. Verkruimel er een over een salade of doop hem in een beetje olijfolie.c7:T51b,### Da dove viene
I taralli sono i biscotti salati ad anello del Sud Italia, e Napoli ne ha una versione tutta sua, famosissima. Si racconta che nel Settecento i fornai, per non buttare i ritagli di pasta del pane, li lavorassero con pepe e grasso, li attorcigliassero ad anello e li infornassero. I venditori ambulanti, i tarallari, li portavano in giro per la città e lungo il mare nelle ceste, tenendoli caldi sotto un panno.

### Come si fa
Il classico tarallo napoletano, detto “sugna e pepe”, nasce da un impasto pepato steso in cordoncini, intrecciati e chiusi ad anello, spesso con le mandorle incastonate in superficie. Gli anelli cuociono a lungo in forno finché diventano dorati e friabili. Ogni produttore ha la sua ricetta: per sapere cosa c'è in questi, fa fede la confezione.

- “Finire a tarallucci e vino” significa chiudere una discussione in amicizia, davanti a un bicchiere.

This red carries the Epomeo IGP designation and comes from Ischia, the volcanic island in the Gulf of Naples. Its name is borrowed from Mount Epomeo, the highest point of the island, an old volcanic peak whose slopes are dotted with vineyards, woods and small villages.

### How it is made
Epomeo Rosso is typically a blend of local red grapes, mainly piedirosso, aglianico and guarnaccia. The result is a medium-bodied, dry red with a delicate fruity nose and soft tannins: a red that stays light on its feet, in the spirit of an island where fish is on the table as often as meat.

### Curiosities
- Piedirosso, one of the grapes in the blend, is known on Ischia by its dialect name Per 'e Palummo, "pigeon's foot".
- The same volcanic activity that shaped Mount Epomeo feeds the island's hot springs, which have made Ischia a spa destination since Roman times.

### How to enjoy it
Serve it slightly below room temperature. It pairs well with seafood, white meat and lighter red meat dishes, and with a simple plate of cured meats and cheese from the counter. It also sits comfortably next to pasta with a light tomato sauce.c9:T497,### Waar het vandaan komt

Falanghina is a white grape of Campania, the region around Naples. It is grown across the region, from the volcanic soils of the Campi Flegrei, just west of the city, to the hills of the Sannio near Benevento, and it has become one of the best-known whites of southern Italy.

### How it is made
This wine is made from 100% falanghina. It is fresh and lively, with notes of citrus, green apple and grapefruit and a delicate mineral finish, a white made to be enjoyed with food rather than to be kept for years.

### Curiosities
- The name is often linked to the Latin *phalanga*, the wooden stake that once held up the vines.
- Falanghina was grown in small quantities for a long time and only returned to the spotlight in the last decades of the 20th century, when producers in Campania began to bottle it on its own.
- Campania is one of the oldest wine regions of Italy: Greek settlers and later the Romans made wine here, and Roman writers praised the wines of the area around Naples.

### How to enjoy it
Serve it chilled. It is at home with fish, seafood and pasta dishes, from spaghetti with clams to pasta with courgette, and works just as well with vegetable dishes and fresh cheeses.cc:T517,### Waar het vandaan komt

It is made from 100% fiano. The wine is elegant and refined, with notes of pear, yellow fruit, hazelnut and delicate flowers. With time in the bottle it can develop hints of honey and exotic fruit, which is part of what makes fiano one of the most respected white grapes of the south.

This white comes from Marsala, on the far western tip of Sicily, a flat, sun-baked coast facing the sea towards Africa. It is an area of wind, light and salt pans, where vineyards have shaped the land for centuries.

### How it is made
It is made from 100% grillo, a white grape that copes well with heat and dry wind. On its own, grillo gives fresh, aromatic whites with good body, made to sit next to the flavours of Sicilian cooking: citrus, herbs, olive oil and fish.

### How it is made
Aglianico is a dark-skinned grape that ripens late. In the cool hills of Irpinia the harvest often runs into late October or even November. The grape is known for reds with deep colour, lively acidity and firm tannins, with dark fruit and a savoury, sometimes spicy side.

- The same grape is behind Taurasi, Irpinia's famous DOCG red, and Aglianico del Vulture in neighbouring Basilicata.
- Because of its structure and ability to age, aglianico is sometimes called the Barolo of the South.

Piedirosso is one of the classic red grapes of Campania, grown around Naples, on the slopes of Vesuvius, in the Campi Flegrei and on the island of Ischia. On Ischia it goes by its dialect name, Per 'e Palummo. This red comes from the island and is made entirely from it.

### How it is made
Made from 100% piedirosso, it is a soft and expressive red. The grape generally gives lighter, gentler wines than aglianico, the other great red variety of the region, which is why it is often enjoyed a little cooler than a big red.

### Curiosities
- Per 'e Palummo means "pigeon's foot": the stalks of the bunches turn red as they ripen, recalling the colour of a pigeon's feet. Piedirosso, "red foot", says the same thing in Italian.
- Across Campania, piedirosso is often blended with aglianico; here it takes the stage on its own.
- On Ischia it is the traditional companion of *coniglio all'ischitana*, rabbit slowly cooked with tomato, garlic and herbs, the island's best-known dish.

### How to enjoy it
Try it with grilled meat, roasts, rabbit and medium-aged cheeses. Served slightly chilled, it can even keep up with richer fish dishes.e7:T4a2,### Waar het vandaan komt

Prosecco comes from north-eastern Italy, from a wide area across Veneto and Friuli Venezia Giulia. The name belongs to the village of Prosecco, on the hills above Trieste, and today it is a protected designation that can only be used for wine from this zone.

### How it is made
The main grape is glera. Most Prosecco gets its bubbles in large pressurised tanks rather than in the bottle, a method that keeps the fruit fresh and the aromas bright. The result is a light, easy sparkling wine: citrus, peach and green apple, carried by fine, lively bubbles.

### Curiosities
- The grape itself was long called prosecco; it was renamed glera when the name became a protected designation.
- The steep hills between Conegliano and Valdobbiadene, the historic heart of Prosecco, are a UNESCO World Heritage site.
- Prosecco is one of the usual bases of the spritz, the Venetian aperitivo that has travelled far beyond Venice.

### How to enjoy it
Serve it cold as an aperitivo, with light bites, seafood or anything salty from the counter. It is also the easy choice when there is something to celebrate, big or small.ea:T4d6,### Waar het vandaan komt
Prosecco komt uit het noordoosten van Italië, uit een groot gebied in Veneto en Friuli Venezia Giulia. De naam komt van het dorp Prosecco, in de heuvels boven Triëst, en is nu een beschermde herkomstbenaming die alleen voor wijn uit deze zone mag worden gebruikt.

### Hoe het gemaakt wordt
De belangrijkste druif is glera. De meeste Prosecco krijgt zijn bubbels in grote tanks onder druk in plaats van in de fles, een methode die het fruit fris en de aroma's helder houdt. Het resultaat is een lichte, toegankelijke mousserende wijn: citrus, perzik en groene appel, gedragen door fijne, levendige bubbels.

### Wetenswaardigheden
- De druif zelf heette lange tijd ook prosecco; hij werd omgedoopt tot glera toen de naam een beschermde herkomstbenaming werd.
- De steile heuvels tussen Conegliano en Valdobbiadene, het historische hart van de Prosecco, staan op de UNESCO-werelderfgoedlijst.
- Prosecco is een van de vaste basissen van de spritz, het Venetiaanse aperitief dat inmiddels ver buiten Venetië bekend is.

Pandoro is the Christmas cake of Verona. Its roots are usually traced to the nadalin, an older star-shaped Christmas bread of the city, and to the golden breads served at the tables of the Venetian nobility, whose name, pan de oro, “golden bread”, may explain its own. The modern pandoro took shape at the end of the nineteenth century, when a Verona confectioner patented the tall star-shaped mould in 1894.

### How it is made
Pandoro is made from a rich, soft leavened dough that rises slowly over many hours, with several rounds of kneading and resting before it is baked. The tall mould with eight points gives it its star shape, and the long rise gives it a light, even crumb and a deep golden colour. Unlike panettone, the classic pandoro has no candied fruit or raisins.

### Curiosities
- It usually comes with a sachet of icing sugar: pour it into the bag, close it and shake, for a snowy finish.
- Cut horizontally, pandoro gives star-shaped slices that can be stacked, slightly turned, into a small Christmas tree.
- In Italy the choice between pandoro and panettone is a friendly family debate every December.

### How to enjoy it
Let it sit at room temperature, dust it with icing sugar and cut it into slices or stars. Serve it with coffee, a sweet wine or a spoonful of mascarpone cream, or toast a slice lightly the next morning.f9:T592,### Waar het vandaan komt

De moderne pandoro kreeg vorm aan het einde van de negentiende eeuw, toen een banketbakker uit Verona in 1894 de hoge stervormige bakvorm patenteerde.

### Hoe het wordt gemaakt
Pandoro wordt gemaakt van een rijk, zacht gerezen deeg dat urenlang langzaam rijst, met meerdere rondes kneden en rusten voordat het de oven in gaat. De hoge vorm met acht punten geeft de ster, en het lange rijzen zorgt voor een luchtige, gelijkmatige kruim en een diepgouden kleur. Anders dan panettone bevat de klassieke pandoro geen gekonfijt fruit of rozijnen.

Panettone is the Christmas cake of Milan. Legends about its birth abound: the best known tells of a cook at the Sforza court whose dessert burned, and of a kitchen boy called Toni who saved the feast with a sweet bread made from what was left, the “pan del Toni”. Whatever the truth, panettone became the symbol of a Milanese Christmas and then of Christmas all over Italy, Naples included, where it shares the table with struffoli and roccocò. This one is dedicated to SSC Napoli, the city's football club.

### How it is made
Traditional panettone takes days. The dough is leavened slowly with a natural starter and kneaded in several stages, then baked in a tall paper case until it rises into its characteristic dome. The classic version holds raisins and candied citrus peel, while modern ones offer creams, chocolate and other fillings.

### Curiosities
- Freshly baked panettoni are hung upside down to cool, so the soft dome does not collapse under its own weight.
- In Milan a last piece saved from Christmas is eaten on 3 February, the feast of San Biagio, as a blessing for the throat.
- Its tall shape comes from the paper mould, a twentieth-century innovation.

### How to enjoy it
Keep it at room temperature and warm it briefly before serving to bring out its aroma. Cut it into vertical wedges and enjoy it with coffee, a glass of spumante or a sweet wine.fc:T5b4,### Waar het vandaan komt

Caciotta is the everyday cheese of central Italy. From Tuscany and Umbria to the Marche and Lazio, farming families traditionally made these small rounds for their own table, using whatever milk the farm had that season. The name comes from “cacio”, the old word for cheese still heard across central and southern Italy, so a caciotta is simply a little cheese.

### How it is made
Milk is warmed and curdled with rennet, the curd is broken into small pieces and pressed into round moulds, then salted. Caciotta matures only briefly, usually a few weeks, which keeps it soft, supple and mild. Flavoured versions are common: chilli, herbs, black pepper or truffle are usually worked into the curd before it goes into the moulds. Here it is peperoncino that brings colour and warmth to a gentle cheese.

### Curiosities
- The same word “cacio” gives its name to cacio e pepe, the Roman pasta of cheese and black pepper.
- Peperoncino is so loved in southern Italy that Calabria dedicates a festival to it every year in Diamante.
- Because it matures for such a short time, caciotta was a cheese farmers could make and eat within the same season.

### How to enjoy it
Slice it for a cheese board next to salami and olives, or put it in a panino with grilled vegetables. It also melts well on warm bread or on a hot slice of grilled polenta.ff:T54f,### Waar het vandaan komt
Caciotta is de alledaagse kaas van Midden-Italië. Van Toscane en Umbrië tot de Marche en Lazio maakten boerenfamilies deze kleine ronde kaasjes van oudsher voor eigen gebruik, met de melk die de boerderij in dat seizoen had. De naam komt van “cacio”, het oude woord voor kaas dat je in Midden- en Zuid-Italië nog steeds hoort. Een caciotta is dus gewoon een kaasje.

De melk wordt verwarmd en met stremsel gestremd, de wrongel wordt in kleine stukjes gebroken, in ronde vormen geperst en gezouten. Caciotta rijpt maar kort, meestal een paar weken, en blijft daardoor zacht, soepel en mild. Varianten met een smaakje zijn populair: chilipeper, kruiden, zwarte peper of truffel worden meestal door de wrongel gewerkt voordat die de vorm in gaat. Hier geeft peperoncino kleur en warmte aan een zachte kaas.

La caciotta è il formaggio di tutti i giorni dell'Italia centrale. Il nome viene da “cacio”, la parola antica per formaggio che si sente ancora in tutto il Centro e il Sud: la caciotta è, semplicemente, un piccolo formaggio.

- Stagionando così poco, la caciotta era un formaggio che i contadini potevano fare e mangiare nella stessa stagione.

Acqualagna is a small town in the Marche, in the hills of the province of Pesaro and Urbino, and it has built its identity around truffles. The woods and river valleys around it yield truffles for much of the year, and every autumn the town hosts a national white truffle fair that draws cooks and buyers from all over Italy. Caciotta, meanwhile, is the everyday farmhouse cheese of central Italy, the Marche included.

### How it is made
To make caciotta, milk is curdled with rennet, the curd is broken up, pressed into small round moulds, salted and left to mature for a short time, usually a few weeks. That keeps it soft and mild, a gentle base for stronger flavours. In truffle versions the truffle is usually worked into the curd before moulding, so its earthy aroma runs through the cheese.

### Curiosities
- Truffles are found by trained dogs that follow the scent under the soil; the hunters are called tartufai.
- Different truffles ripen in different seasons, which is why truffle towns like Acqualagna talk about truffles all year round.
- “Cacio”, the root of caciotta, is the old central Italian word for cheese.

### How to enjoy it

Pastiera is the Easter cake of Naples, and few desserts carry as many legends. One tells that the siren Parthenope, who lived in the gulf, received gifts of flour, ricotta, wheat, spices and orange blossom from the people of Naples, and that the gods blended them into the first pastiera. Another credits the nuns of the San Gregorio Armeno convent with perfecting the recipe centuries ago.

### How it is made
The classic pastiera has a shortcrust shell filled with wheat berries cooked until soft, mixed with ricotta, sugar, candied citrus peel and orange blossom water, sometimes with a touch of cinnamon. Strips of pastry form a lattice on top, and the cake bakes slowly to a deep golden colour. Recipes vary from family to family and from one maker to another.

### Curiosities
- Tradition says the lattice has seven strips, and many see in them the plan of the old city's streets.
- Neapolitans say that a queen known for never smiling finally smiled when she tasted pastiera.
- Pastiera is usually made a few days before Easter, since it tastes better after resting.

### How to enjoy it
Serve it at room temperature, in generous slices, perhaps with a dusting of icing sugar. It goes well with coffee in the morning, or with a small glass of sweet wine after Easter lunch.105:T554,### Waar het vandaan komt
Pastiera is de paastaart van Napels, en weinig desserts hebben zoveel legendes. Eén ervan vertelt dat de sirene Parthenope, die in de golf woonde, van de Napolitanen geschenken kreeg: meel, ricotta, graan, specerijen en oranjebloesem, en dat de goden die mengden tot de eerste pastiera. Een andere legende schrijft aan de nonnen van het klooster van San Gregorio Armeno toe dat zij het recept eeuwen geleden vervolmaakten.

### Hoe het wordt gemaakt
De klassieke pastiera heeft een bodem van zanddeeg, gevuld met zachtgekookte tarwekorrels, gemengd met ricotta, suiker, gekonfijte citrusschil en oranjebloesemwater, soms met een vleugje kaneel. Reepjes deeg vormen een rooster bovenop, waarna de taart langzaam goudbruin bakt. Het recept verschilt van familie tot familie en van maker tot maker.

Provolone del Monaco belongs to the Monti Lattari, the green mountains that run from the hills above Naples down to the Sorrento peninsula and the Amalfi coast. Dairy farming there is old and small in scale, with cows grazing steep slopes between lemon groves and chestnut woods. The cheese takes its name from those hills and from the people who made it.

### How it is made
Provolone del Monaco is a pasta filata cheese: the curd is heated and stretched by hand in hot water until it turns smooth and elastic, then shaped into a large pear or melon form. The cheeses are tied with cord and hung in pairs to mature for months in cool rooms, where the rind turns golden and the paste firms up and develops a rounder, gently piquant taste.

### Curiosities
- The name is usually explained with a story: cheesemakers crossed the gulf to the Naples markets by boat before dawn, wrapped in heavy cloaks against the cold, and the traders called them “the monks”.
- The Agerolese cow, a small breed of the Lattari hills, is closely tied to the tradition of this cheese.
- It is the cheese of spaghetti alla Nerano, the Amalfi coast dish of fried courgettes melted into a creamy sauce.

### How to enjoy it
Cut it into thin slices and eat it with good bread and a glass of red wine, or with pears. Grate or shave it over pasta, or melt it into courgettes the Nerano way.108:T5af,### Waar het vandaan komt

Focaccia genovese comes from Genoa, on the Ligurian coast of north-west Italy, where it is simply called fugassa. It is part of daily life there: people buy it from the bakery in the morning, eat it at school break or with a glass of white wine in the evening.

### How it is made
The dough is made with flour, water, yeast, salt and olive oil, stretched into a low tray and left to rise. Before baking, the baker presses fingertips into the dough to make its typical dimples and pours over an emulsion of oil, water and salt, which collects in the hollows. This version is topped with cherry tomatoes.

### Curiosities
- In Genoa many people dip focaccia into their morning cappuccino or caffelatte.
- The dimples are not decoration: they hold the oil and brine, so the top stays soft and the base crisp.
- Liguria has other famous focacce too, such as the thin, cheese-filled one from Recco.

### How to enjoy it
Warm it briefly in the oven so the crust crisps up again, then cut it into squares. Serve it as it is with an aperitivo, split it and fill it with mortadella or stracciatella, or put it on the table with antipasti to share.10b:T4b6,### Waar het vandaan komt
Focaccia genovese komt uit Genua, aan de Ligurische kust in het noordwesten van Italië, waar men het gewoon fugassa noemt. Het hoort er bij het dagelijks leven: mensen halen het 's ochtends bij de bakker, eten het in de schoolpauze of 's avonds bij een glas witte wijn.

### Hoe het gemaakt wordt
Het deeg wordt gemaakt van bloem, water, gist, zout en olijfolie, uitgespreid in een lage bakplaat en laten rijzen. Voor het bakken drukt de bakker er met de vingertoppen de typische kuiltjes in en giet er een emulsie van olie, water en zout over, die zich in de kuiltjes verzamelt. Deze versie is belegd met kerstomaatjes.

### Weetjes
- In Genua dopen veel mensen hun focaccia 's ochtends in de cappuccino of caffelatte.
- De kuiltjes zijn geen versiering: ze houden olie en pekel vast, zodat de bovenkant zacht blijft en de bodem knapperig.
- Ligurië kent nog meer beroemde focacce, zoals de dunne, met kaas gevulde uit Recco.

Burrata comes from Puglia, the heel of Italy, and is usually linked to the area around Andria in the early twentieth century. It was a clever way to use the scraps of stretched curd left over from making mozzarella, and it soon became one of the region's best-loved fresh cheeses.

### How it is made
The cheesemaker stretches curd in hot water, as for mozzarella, and shapes it into a thin pouch. The pouch is filled with stracciatella, shreds of stretched curd soaked in cream, and closed at the top with a small knot. The result is firm on the outside and soft and creamy inside.

### Curiosities
- The name comes from burro, butter, a nod to how rich it is.
- Burrata was traditionally wrapped in green asphodel leaves, which showed at a glance how fresh it was.
- It is meant to be eaten within a day or two, when the filling is at its creamiest.

### How to enjoy it
Let it sit out of the fridge for a while, then cut it open at the table and let the filling spill out. Serve it with ripe tomatoes, olive oil, a pinch of salt and bread, on focaccia, or on top of a simple pasta.10e:T478,### Waar het vandaan komt
Burrata komt uit Apulië, de hak van Italië, en wordt meestal in verband gebracht met de omgeving van Andria in het begin van de twintigste eeuw. Het was een slimme manier om de restjes getrokken wrongel van het mozzarella maken te gebruiken, en al snel werd het een van de geliefdste verse kazen van de streek.

### Hoe het gemaakt wordt
De kaasmaker trekt de wrongel in heet water, net als bij mozzarella, en vormt er een dun buideltje van. Dat wordt gevuld met stracciatella, reepjes getrokken wrongel gedrenkt in room, en bovenaan met een knoopje gesloten. Het resultaat is stevig vanbuiten en zacht en romig vanbinnen.

### Weetjes
- De naam komt van burro, boter, een knipoog naar hoe rijk hij is.
- Vroeger werd burrata in groene affodilbladeren gewikkeld; zo zag je meteen hoe vers hij was.
- Hij is bedoeld om binnen een à twee dagen te eten, als de vulling op haar romigst is.

### Zo geniet je ervan
Laat hem even buiten de koelkast staan, snijd hem aan tafel open en laat de vulling eruit lopen. Serveer met rijpe tomaten, olijfolie, een snufje zout en brood, op focaccia of bovenop een eenvoudige pasta.10f:T441,### Da dove viene
La burrata viene dalla Puglia, il tacco d'Italia, e di solito si lega alla zona di Andria all'inizio del Novecento. Era un modo intelligente per recuperare i ritagli di pasta filata avanzati dalla produzione della mozzarella, e presto è diventata uno dei formaggi freschi più amati della regione.

### Come si fa
Il casaro fila la cagliata in acqua calda, come per la mozzarella, e la modella in un sottile sacchetto. Lo riempie di stracciatella, sfilacci di pasta filata immersi nella panna, e lo chiude in alto con un piccolo nodo. Il risultato è compatto fuori e morbido e cremoso dentro.

### Curiosità
- Il nome viene da burro, per la sua ricchezza.
- Un tempo la burrata si avvolgeva nelle foglie verdi di asfodelo, che mostravano a colpo d'occhio quanto fosse fresca.
- Va mangiata entro uno o due giorni, quando il ripieno è al massimo della cremosità.

### Come gustarlo
Lasciala un po' fuori dal frigo, poi aprila a tavola e lascia uscire il ripieno. Servila con pomodori maturi, olio, un pizzico di sale e pane, sulla focaccia o sopra una pasta semplice.110:T4d0,### Where it comes from
Caciocavallo is one of the oldest cheeses of southern Italy, made from Campania and Molise down to Puglia, Basilicata, Calabria and Sicily. Its gourd-like shape, with a little knob at the top, is a familiar sight in the salumerie of the south, where the cheeses hang from the ceiling in rows.

### How it is made
Like mozzarella, it is a pasta filata cheese: the curd is stretched in hot water, shaped by hand and tied at the neck with a string. The cheese is then salted and aged. Younger caciocavallo is mild and supple; with longer ageing it becomes firmer, more savoury and slightly piquant.

### Curiosities
- The name is usually explained by the way the cheeses are hung in pairs, a cavallo, astride a pole.
- In Campania and Basilicata there is a tradition of caciocavallo impiccato: the cheese is hung over hot embers and the melting part is scraped onto bread.
- Caciocavallo Silano, from the south, is one of Italy's protected cheeses.

### How to enjoy it
Serve it in slices with bread, olives and cured meats, or with pears and a little honey. Try thick slices seared in a hot pan until golden and soft, or melt it over roasted vegetables or into a panino. A southern red wine makes a good match.111:T53b,### Waar het vandaan komt
Caciocavallo is een van de oudste kazen van Zuid-Italië en wordt gemaakt van Campanië en Molise tot Apulië, Basilicata, Calabrië en Sicilië. Zijn kalebasvorm, met een klein knopje bovenop, is een vertrouwd beeld in de salumerie van het zuiden, waar de kazen in rijen aan het plafond hangen.

### Hoe het gemaakt wordt
Net als mozzarella is het een pasta filata-kaas: de wrongel wordt in heet water getrokken, met de hand gevormd en bij de hals met een touwtje afgebonden. Daarna wordt de kaas gezouten en gerijpt. Jonge caciocavallo is mild en soepel; hoe langer hij rijpt, hoe steviger, hartiger en licht pikanter hij wordt.

### Weetjes
- De naam wordt meestal verklaard door de manier waarop de kazen per twee worden opgehangen, a cavallo, schrijlings over een stok.
- In Campanië en Basilicata bestaat de traditie van caciocavallo impiccato: de kaas hangt boven gloeiende kolen en het gesmolten deel wordt op brood geschraapt.
- Caciocavallo Silano uit het zuiden is een van de beschermde kazen van Italië.

Il caciocavallo è uno dei formaggi più antichi del Sud Italia e si produce dalla Campania e dal Molise fino a Puglia, Basilicata, Calabria e Sicilia. La sua forma a pera, con una piccola testina in cima, è una presenza familiare nelle salumerie del Sud, dove le forme pendono dal soffitto in fila.

### Come si fa
Come la mozzarella, è un formaggio a pasta filata: la cagliata si fila in acqua calda, si modella a mano e si lega al collo con uno spago. Poi il formaggio viene salato e stagionato. Da giovane il caciocavallo è dolce ed elastico; con una stagionatura più lunga diventa più compatto, sapido e leggermente piccante.

### Curiosità
- Il nome si spiega di solito con il modo in cui le forme vengono appese a coppie, a cavallo di una pertica.
- In Campania e Basilicata c'è la tradizione del caciocavallo impiccato: il formaggio si appende sopra la brace e la parte che si scioglie si raccoglie sul pane.
- Il Caciocavallo Silano, del Sud, è uno dei formaggi italiani a denominazione protetta.

### Come gustarlo
Servilo a fette con pane, olive e salumi, oppure con pere e un filo di miele. Prova fette spesse scottate in padella rovente finché non diventano dorate e morbide, o fallo fondere su verdure arrosto o in un panino. Un rosso del Sud è l'abbinamento giusto.113:T46c,### Where it comes from
Soppressata is one of the classic salami of southern Italy. Calabria, Basilicata, Puglia and Campania each have their own version, and in Calabria it even carries a protected designation. Every family and every valley used to have its own balance of meat and seasoning, and many still do.

### How it is made
The pork is cut by knife or coarsely ground, seasoned and stuffed into a natural casing. The salame is then pressed, traditionally under boards or weights, which gives it its flattened shape and its oval slice. After that it hangs to dry and age for weeks or months, depending on size and style.

### Curiosities
- The name comes from soppressare, to press down.
- In Tuscany a product with a similar name is something quite different: a cooked preparation, not a cured salame.
- Some versions are mild and some are spicy; the tradition changes from one area to the next.

### How to enjoy it
Slice it not too thin, so you can taste the coarse texture. Put it on a board with bread, olives and a firm cheese such as pecorino, or tuck it into a panino. A robust red wine from the south suits it well.114:T487,### Waar het vandaan komt
Soppressata is een van de klassieke salami's van Zuid-Italië. Calabrië, Basilicata, Apulië en Campanië hebben elk hun eigen versie, en in Calabrië heeft hij zelfs een beschermde benaming. Vroeger had elke familie en elk dal een eigen verhouding tussen vlees en kruiding, en dat is vaak nog zo.

### Hoe het gemaakt wordt
Het varkensvlees wordt met het mes gesneden of grof gemalen, gekruid en in een natuurdarm gevuld. Vervolgens hangt hij weken of maanden te drogen en te rijpen, afhankelijk van formaat en stijl.

- De naam komt van soppressare, platdrukken.

La soppressata è uno dei salami classici del Sud Italia. Calabria, Basilicata, Puglia e Campania hanno ciascuna la propria versione, e in Calabria ha anche una denominazione protetta. Un tempo ogni famiglia e ogni valle aveva il suo equilibrio tra carne e condimento, e spesso è ancora così.

Scamorza is a cheese of southern Italy, especially Campania, Molise, Abruzzo and Puglia. It belongs to the family of pasta filata cheeses, together with mozzarella, provola and caciocavallo, and for generations it has been a staple of kitchens across the south.

### How it is made
The curd is stretched in hot water until smooth and elastic, then shaped into a small pear. A string is tied around the top, creating the little head that makes scamorza easy to recognise, and the cheese is hung to dry for a short time. That drying makes it firmer and less milky than mozzarella. Scamorza comes in two styles: bianca, plain and mild, and affumicata, smoked, with a golden rind and a gentle smoky aroma.

### Curiosities
- The name is often linked to the southern expression capa mozza, cut-off head, from the way the top is pinched.
- Because it is drier than mozzarella, scamorza melts without releasing much water, which makes it ideal for cooking.
- Scamorza and provola are close cousins; scamorza is usually smaller and shaped like a pear.

### How to enjoy it
Cut it into thick slices and grill them in a hot pan until golden outside and soft inside. It is also excellent melted on pizza, in a panino, over roasted vegetables or baked with pasta. The plain one suits lighter dishes; the smoked one adds depth to potatoes and aubergine.117:T5b2,### Waar het vandaan komt
Scamorza is een kaas uit Zuid-Italië, vooral Campanië, Molise, de Abruzzen en Apulië. Hij hoort bij de familie van pasta filata-kazen, samen met mozzarella, provola en caciocavallo, en is al generaties een vaste waarde in de keukens van het zuiden.

### Hoe het gemaakt wordt
De wrongel wordt in heet water getrokken tot hij glad en elastisch is en daarna in de vorm van een peertje gebracht. Rond de bovenkant wordt een touwtje geknoopt, wat het kopje vormt waaraan je scamorza herkent, en de kaas hangt kort te drogen. Daardoor is hij steviger en minder melkachtig dan mozzarella. Scamorza bestaat in twee stijlen: bianca, naturel en mild, en affumicata, gerookt, met een goudkleurige korst en een zacht rokerig aroma.

### Weetjes
- De naam wordt vaak verbonden met de zuidelijke uitdrukking capa mozza, afgeknipt hoofd, naar de manier waarop de bovenkant wordt afgeknepen.
- Omdat hij droger is dan mozzarella, smelt scamorza zonder veel vocht los te laten; ideaal om mee te koken.
- Scamorza en provola zijn naaste familie; scamorza is meestal kleiner en heeft de vorm van een peer.

Nocellara is one of the best-known olive varieties of Sicily. The island has grown olives since antiquity, and the Nocellara family, which includes the Nocellara del Belice from the west of the island and the Nocellara Etnea from the slopes of Etna, is prized both for oil and for the table.

### How it is made
Table olives cannot be eaten straight from the tree: fresh olives are very bitter. They are cured, traditionally in brine, until the bitterness softens and the flesh becomes pleasant to eat. Picked green, Nocellara olives keep a bright colour, a firm bite and a mild, buttery taste.

### Curiosities
- Nocellara del Belice is a protected table olive, one of the few in Italy.
- In Sicily green olives are often cracked and dressed with garlic, herbs and chilli to make olive schiacciate.
- The same variety is also pressed for oil, giving a fresh, green and gently fruity olive oil.

### How to enjoy it
Serve them at room temperature with a glass of wine or a spritz, alongside cheese and cured meats. Add them to salads, to a pan of fish or chicken, or to a tomato sauce. Keep any leftovers covered in their liquid.11a:T4a8,### Waar het vandaan komt
Nocellara is een van de bekendste olijvenrassen van Sicilië. ### Hoe het gemaakt wordt
Tafelolijven kun je niet zo van de boom eten: verse olijven zijn heel bitter. Ze worden ingelegd, traditioneel in pekel, tot de bitterheid verzacht en het vruchtvlees aangenaam wordt. Groen geplukt houden Nocellara-olijven een heldere kleur, een stevige beet en een milde, boterachtige smaak.

### Weetjes
- Nocellara del Belice is een beschermde tafelolijf, een van de weinige in Italië.

Guanciale takes its name from guancia, the Italian word for cheek. It belongs to central Italy, above all Lazio, Umbria and Abruzzo, where the pork cheek has long been cured instead of going to waste. In Rome it is the ingredient that gives the city's best-known pasta dishes their depth.

### How it is made
The cheek is trimmed, rubbed with salt and usually black pepper, sometimes with other spices, then left to cure and dry for several weeks. What you get is a firm piece with wide bands of fat and thin streaks of meat. Its fat is softer and more fragrant than that of pancetta, and it melts differently in the pan.

### Curiosities
- Carbonara, amatriciana and gricia are traditionally made with guanciale; pancetta is the usual substitute outside Italy.
- Gricia is often described as an amatriciana without tomato.
- Cooked slowly, the fat turns translucent and the edges crisp up, which is why many cooks start it in a cold pan.

### How to enjoy it
Cut it into strips or small cubes, trim off any hard rind and let it render slowly in a dry pan. Toss your pasta in the fat it releases, then finish with Pecorino Romano and plenty of black pepper. Very thin slices are also good on warm bread.11d:T517,### Waar het vandaan komt
Guanciale dankt zijn naam aan guancia, het Italiaanse woord voor wang. Het hoort bij Midden-Italië, vooral Lazio, Umbrië en de Abruzzen, waar de varkenswang al heel lang wordt gezouten en gedroogd in plaats van weggegooid. In Rome is het het ingrediënt dat de bekendste pastagerechten van de stad hun diepte geeft.

### Hoe het gemaakt wordt
De wang wordt bijgesneden, ingewreven met zout en meestal zwarte peper, soms met andere specerijen, en daarna enkele weken te rijpen en drogen gelegd. Het resultaat is een stevig stuk met brede banen vet en dunne lagen vlees. Het vet is zachter en geuriger dan dat van pancetta en smelt anders in de pan.

### Weetjes
- Carbonara, amatriciana en gricia worden traditioneel met guanciale gemaakt; buiten Italië gebruikt men vaak pancetta als vervanger.
- Gricia wordt vaak omschreven als een amatriciana zonder tomaat.
- Langzaam gebakken wordt het vet glazig en worden de randjes knapperig; daarom beginnen veel koks in een koude pan.

### Zo geniet je ervan
Snijd het in reepjes of blokjes, haal een eventuele harde zwoerd eraf en laat het langzaam uitbakken in een droge pan. Schep de pasta om in het vet dat vrijkomt en maak af met Pecorino Romano en flink wat zwarte peper. Flinterdunne plakjes zijn ook heerlijk op warm brood.11e:T50a,### Da dove viene
Il guanciale prende il nome dalla guancia del maiale. È un salume del Centro Italia, soprattutto di Lazio, Umbria e Abruzzo, dove la guancia si stagiona da sempre invece di andare sprecata. A Roma è l'ingrediente che dà profondità ai piatti di pasta più famosi della città.

### Come si fa
La guancia viene rifilata, massaggiata con sale e di solito pepe nero, a volte con altre spezie, e poi lasciata stagionare e asciugare per alcune settimane. Il risultato è un pezzo compatto, con ampie fasce di grasso e sottili venature di carne. Il suo grasso è più morbido e profumato di quello della pancetta e in padella si scioglie in modo diverso.

### Curiosità
- Carbonara, amatriciana e gricia si fanno per tradizione con il guanciale; fuori dall'Italia lo si sostituisce spesso con la pancetta.
- La gricia viene spesso descritta come un'amatriciana senza pomodoro.
- Cotto piano, il grasso diventa trasparente e i bordi croccanti: per questo molti partono a padella fredda.

Caciocavallo is one of the oldest cheeses of southern Italy, made from Campania and Molise down to Puglia, Basilicata, Calabria and Sicily. Its gourd-like shape, with a little knob at the top, is recognisable in every salumeria of the south, where the cheeses hang from the ceiling in rows.

### How it is made
Like mozzarella, it is a pasta filata cheese: the curd is stretched in hot water, then shaped by hand into its typical form and tied at the neck. It is salted and then aged, from a few weeks to many months. This one is finished in a cave, where cool, humid air helps the rind develop and the flavour grow deeper and more complex.

### Curiosities
- The name is usually explained by the way the cheeses are hung in pairs, a cavallo, astride a pole.
- In Campania and Basilicata there is a tradition of caciocavallo impiccato: the cheese is hung over hot embers and the melting part is scraped onto bread.
- Caciocavallo Silano, from the south, is one of Italy's protected cheeses.

### How to enjoy it
Serve it in slices or chunks with bread, pears or a spoon of honey. Grill thick slices in a hot pan until they soften and colour at the edges, and eat them straight away. A structured southern red wine suits the aged flavour.120:T53d,### Waar het vandaan komt
